Poroelastic flow across a permeable interface: a Hamilton’s principle approach and its finite element implementation
Francesco Costanzo, Mohammad Jannesari, Beatrice Ghitti

Abstract
We consider fluid flow across a permeable interface within a deformable porous medium. We use mixture theory. The mixture’s constituents are assumed to be incompressible in their pure form. We use Hamilton’s principle to obtain the governing equations, and we propose a corresponding finite element implementation. The filtration velocity and the pore pressure are allowed to be discontinuous across the interface while some control of these discontinuities is built into the interfacial constitutive behavior. To facilitate the practical implementation of the formulation in a finite element scheme, we introduce a Lagrange multiplier field over the interface for the explicit enforcement of the jump condition of the balance of mass. Our formulation appears to recover some basic results from the literature. The novelty of the work is the formulation of an approach that can accommodate specific constitutive assumptions pertaining to the behavior of the interface that do not necessarily imply the continuity of the filtration velocity and/or of the pore pressure across it.



keywords
Poroelasticity; Jump Conditions; Hamilton’s Principle; Finite Element Method.

\ccode

AMS Subject Classification: 74A99, 76S05, 74S05, 76-10

1
Introduction

This paper is motivated by our interest in modeling aspects of brain physiology. Poroelasticity can be effective in modeling
IF
(
IF
) flow through brain parenchyma and its interaction with the
CSF
(
CSF
) circulation. While this paper is not about brain physiology, we briefly touch upon the problem of identifying the pathways and drivers of waste clearance in the brain to provide some context for what we present herein.

The accumulation of potentially neurotoxic waste products, such as amyloid-
\beta
, in the brain
ECS
(
ECS
) has been linked to the pathogenesis of neurodegenerative disorders like Alzheimer’s disease.
[
47
]
The brain does not have a conventional lymphatic system
[
1
]
responsible for clearing products of metabolism and neurotransmission,
[
41
,
43
]
and the identification of clearance pathways and driving mechanisms remains a poorly understood aspect of brain physiology and pathology.
[
25
,
26
,
2
]

The brain and spinal cord are surrounded by
CSF
, which flows through a system of fluid-filled communicating spaces including the
SAS
(
SAS
), ventricular system, central canal of the spinal cord, and the
PVS
(
PVS
) surrounding cerebral vessels. Transport of waste by
CSF
flow is acknowledged as a key process in maintaining brain function. Transport of metabolites through parenchyma has traditionally been attributed to Fickian diffusion
[
49
,
50
,
52
,
53
]
eventually leading to mixing with
CSF
and draining into the peripheral lymphatics in the neck.
[
10
,
11
]
More recently, the work by Iliff, Nedergaard, and co-workers
[
31
,
29
,
32
,
30
]
has challenged this understanding, pointing to the existence of a directional flow of fluid into and out of the
CNS
(
CNS
). This
circulation
has been dubbed the
glymphatic system
and is characterized by a significant convective transport and exchange between
IF
and
CSF
along the
PVS
. However, several aspects of the glymphatic hypothesis remain controversial
[
25
,
26
,
2
]
: little is known about the mechanisms and forces driving fluid movement. Various drivers of the glymphatic flow have been proposed in the literature, such as arterial pulsation,
[
32
,
40
,
7
]
functional hyperemia,
[
34
,
33
]
respiration,
[
35
]
and body posture.
[
36
]

The transport phenomena at hand are characterized by flow through parenchyma that has both convective and diffusive features. Furthermore, this flow can cross a variety of permeable membranes, such as the glia limitans and the pia mater (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Hladky2022The-glymphatic-). These membranes have different cellular structures and can play different roles, including that of regulating osmotic pressures. To simultaneously account for both diffusive and convective flows, we had adopted
[
16
,
33
]
a poroelasticity theory based on mixture theory.
[
8
,
9
]
In this paper, we focus on interface conditions at permeable interfaces with an eye toward the possibility of explicitly reflecting interfacial properties in our model.

There is a vast literature on the conditions at the interface between a porous medium and a fluid (see, e.g., in Refs.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit,Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas, and
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0). This literature concerns both modeling aspects (e.g., Refs.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit,Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas, and
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0) as well as the numerical implementation of such conditions (e.g., Refs.
\refcite
Lee2019A-Mixed-Finite-,Causemann2022Human-Intracran, and
\refcite
Ruiz-Baier2022The-Biot-Stokes). It is well-known that the jump conditions of the balance laws do not imply or demand that the fluid velocity field or the pore pressure be continuous across the interface.
[
8
]
It is also well-known that a
BVP
(
BVP
) without sufficient constraints on said discontinuities is generally ill-posed (e.g., see discussion in Ref.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit) and such that the ill-posedness has different manifestations depending on the fluid’s constitutive behavior on the two sides of the interface. Therefore, complementing the jump conditions of the balance laws with some additional interface conditions is often essential for the formulation of a well-posed
BVP
. Provided that several approaches have been proposed in the literature, by and large, the most commonly adopted interface conditions (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas) are kinematic in nature as they prescribe continuity for the pore pressure field and the tangential component of the filtration velocity. For mixtures of incompressible materials, the continuity of the tangential component of the filtration velocity, along with the requirement that its normal component be continuous as a consequence of the balance of mass, yields a continuity condition on the filtration velocity as a whole, leaving the fluid velocity field discontinuous. Such continuity requirement is fairly straightforward to implement in practice whenever the field equations are written in terms of filtration velocity, as opposed to the fluid velocity. However, this type of formulation may not always be appropriate for the modeling of a particular problem. This said, and much more importantly, prescribing the continuity of certain fields across the interface is tantamount to prescribing an
implicit
constitutive response to the interface. This idea is brought out by the analysis in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 where the authors use Hamilton’s principle
[
6
]
to derive the problem’s governing equations and interface conditions. In this approach, one must define an action potential and, when dissipation is present, a Rayleigh pseudo-potential. In turn, this requires an explicit model of all energy storage and dissipation mechanisms in the system. As such, the interface constitutive behavior is explicitly modeled and the associated interface conditions are derived by a choice of test functions that appropriately respects the kinematic assumptions underlying the model. In doing so, dell’Isola
et al.
[
18
]
obtained interface conditions that had not previously appeared in the literature but that transparently translated the constitutive behavior of the interface and its relation to the constitutive behavior of the surrounding materials, regardless of whether they are both poroelastic, or one being poroelastic and the other being pure fluid.

We found the work presented in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 to provide the most appropriate setting for our interest. This said, we could not find a numerical implementation of such framework, especially for the case in which the constituents are incompressible in their pure form. Furthermore, to obtain the interface conditions pertaining to the motion of the fluid relative to the solid, the authors of Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 designed two
ad hoc
test functions for each of two different components of the interface conditions and distinguishing the cases with and without mass transport across the interface. While we find said construction rather clever, we also find it somewhat difficult to implement it practically within common
FEM
(
FEM
) platforms. With this in mind, the objectives of this work are (
i
) to propose an adaptation of the variational approach in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 to the case in which each of the mixture constituents is incompressible in its pure form, and (
ii
) to propose a practical implementation of the approach using a
FEM
that allows the use of commonly available discontinuous representations of fluid velocity fields.

The paper is organized as follows. First, in Sec.
2
, we present the essential notation and basic kinematics needed to describe our model. In Sec.
3
, we then discuss the relevant balance laws following the traditional approach in Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 and we derive a corresponding energy estimate. In Sec.
4
, we derive the field equations using Hamilton’s principle and offer a comparison with the previously derived governing equations. In this section we also propose a practical
FEM
implementation of our equations. In Sec.
5
, we discuss some examples. In Sec.
6
, we conclude the paper with a summary and discussion.

2
Basic definitions and kinematics

Here we describe the motion of our system. Our treatment is rooted in Refs.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 and
\refcite
Bowen1980Incompressible-0 and we adopt the kinematic and analytical framework in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, which we adapt to the case of a biphasic mixture of incompressible constituents. For completeness, basic definitions have been placed in Appendix
A
.

2.1
The physical system and its kinematics

We consider a mixture with a solid and a fluid phase. The mixture’s current configuration is
B(t)\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
, while
B_{\mathrm{s}}\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
and
B_{\mathrm{f}}\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
are the reference configurations of the solid and fluid phases, respectively. The symbols
\bm{\mathbf{x}}
,
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}}
, and
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}}
denote points in
B(t)
,
B_{\mathrm{s}}
, and
B_{\mathrm{f}}
, respectively. Referring to the notation in Eq. (
82
),
e
,
s
, and
f
indicate
B(t)
,
B_{\mathrm{s}}
, and
B_{\mathrm{f}}
, respectively.

B(t)
is the common image of the solid and fluid motions, respectively

\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}:B_{\mathrm{s}}\times\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\to B(t)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}:B_{\mathrm{f}}\times\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\to B(t),

(1)

which are expected to be diffeomorphisms except, possibly, across a permeable interface
\mathcal{S}(t)\subset B(t)
separating two (or more) porous domains and/or a porous and a purely fluid domain.

If
\mathcal{S}(t)
separates two distinct porous domains,
\mathcal{S}(t)
is assumed to be a coherent interface convected by a globally continuous solid motion. If
\mathcal{S}(t)
separates a porous domain from a purely fluid domain,
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
is understood to be continuously extended in the fluid domain. In all cases,
\mathcal{S}(t)
is the image under
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
of a smooth not self-intersecting
fixed
submanifold
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}\subset B_{\mathrm{s}}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
either cuts through
B_{\mathrm{s}}
intersecting the boundary of
B_{\mathrm{s}}
or is closed and completely contained in
B_{\mathrm{s}}
.
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
is the inverse image of
\mathcal{S}(t)
under
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
.

Next, away from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
, the gradients (the meaning of
\nabla
is context dependent, cf. Appendix
A
) of
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
are

\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\coloneqq\nabla\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t)\coloneqq\nabla\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t),

(2)

with determinants
J_{\mathrm{s}}
and
J_{\mathrm{f}}
, respectively, which are taken to be strictly positive. Similarly, the solid and fluid material velocity fields are, respectively,

\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\coloneqq\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t)\coloneqq\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t).

(3)

In Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, it is posited that the motions
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
are diffeomorphisms away from the interface, but can suffer jump discontinuities in their space–time gradients so as to have the Hadamard properties.
[
18
]
A general discussion of the subject can be found in Refs.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0,Truesdell1965The-Non-Linear-0, and
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0. Here, we start with following the same kinematic assumption for
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
as in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, so that
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
is continuous across the interface. We will also assume that, away from the interface,
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
is a diffeomorphism. However, we will allow the fluid material velocity field to be discontinuous across the interface while only restricted to the requirements imposed by the jump condition of the mass balance. The kinematic constraint originating with the latter will be imposed via a Lagrange multiplier in Sec.
4.1
.

We denote by
\bm{\mathbf{m}}_{\mathrm{s}}
the unit normal orienting
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
. The corresponding normal orienting
\mathcal{S}(t)
is
\bm{\mathbf{m}}
, which is given by

\bm{\mathbf{m}}\coloneqq\biggl(\frac{J_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle-\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}_{\mathrm{s}}}{\|J_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle-\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}_{\mathrm{s}}\|}\biggr)^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},

(4)

and the normal velocity of
\mathcal{S}(t)
is the unique scalar field

c_{\mathcal{S}(t)}:\mathcal{S}(t)\to\mathbb{R},\quad c_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\coloneqq\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}}.

(5)

In addition to the maps
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
, following Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, we introduce the following map

\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}:B_{\mathrm{s}}\to B_{\mathrm{f}},\quad\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\coloneqq\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{-1}\circ\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}.

(6)

The function
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)
, which is well-defined away from the interface, identifies the fluid particle
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}}
that, at time
t
, occupies the same position in the mixture as the solid material particle
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}}
. This map encodes the relative motion of the fluid with respect to the solid. Letting

\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}\coloneqq\nabla\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}},\quad J_{\mathrm{sf}}=\det\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\coloneqq\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}},

(7)

it can be shown that

\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}^{-1},\quad J_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=J_{\mathrm{s}}/J_{\mathrm{sf}},\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}-\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{sf}}.

(8)

If the solid and fluid are submanifolds of an all-containing Euclidean manifold, the displacement of the solid and fluid phases, denoted by
\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{f}}
, respectively, are defined as

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\coloneqq\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)-\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t)\coloneqq\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t)-\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}}.

(9)

We note that

\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}-\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}.

(10)

The material accelerations of the solid and fluid phases, denoted by
\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}
, respectively, are

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\coloneqq\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t)\coloneqq\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}},t).

(11)

3
Balance of mass and momentum

Here we summarize the presentation in Refs.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 and
\refcite
Bowen1980Incompressible-0.

3.1
Balance of mass

In their pure form, the fluid and the solid phases are assumed to be incompressible with mass densities given by the constants
\rho_{\mathrm{f}}^{*}
and
\rho_{\mathrm{s}}^{*}
, respectively.

Let

\phi_{\mathrm{s}}:B(t)\to[0,1]\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{\mathrm{f}}:B(t)\to[0,1]

(12)

denote the volume fractions of the solid and fluid, respectively, subject to the
saturation condition

\phi_{\mathrm{s}}+\phi_{\mathrm{f}}=1.

(13)

Then, the mass densities of the solid and the fluid over
B(t)
(i.e., in the mixture) are, respectively,

\rho_{\mathrm{s}}=\rho_{\mathrm{s}}^{*}\phi_{\mathrm{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\rho_{\mathrm{f}}=\rho_{\mathrm{f}}^{*}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}.

(14)

In the absence of chemical reactions, the mass balance in
B(t)
requires that

\partial_{t}\rho_{a}+\nabla\!\cdot\!(\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\partial_{t}\phi_{a}+\nabla\!\cdot\!(\phi_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})=0,

(15)

for each species
a=\mathrm{s},\mathrm{f}
.
In view of Eq. (
13
), the motion of the mixture is governed by the following (single) constraint:

\nabla\!\cdot\!(\phi_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(17)

The field
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{va}}
is the volume average velocity of the mixture, whereas
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}
is the filtration velocity. Using Eqs. (
17
), Eq. (
16
) can also be written as

\nabla\!\cdot\!\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{va}}=0\quad\text{or}\quad\nabla\!\cdot\!\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\nabla\!\cdot\!\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}=0.

(18)

As
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}
may not be continuous across
\mathcal{S}(t)
, we also need to state the jump condition of the balance of mass. We assume that the interface does not support the production or removal of mass. Hence, using traditional arguments (cf. Refs.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 and
\refcite
GurtinFried_2010_The-Mechanics_0), at every point on
\mathcal{S}(t)
we must have

\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{va}}\rrbracket\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}}=0,

(19)

where the second of Eqs. (
19
) is a consequence of the continuity of
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}
.

The second of Eqs. (
15
) and the assumed regularity of the motions away from the interface imply the existence of referential forms of the volume fraction distributions for the solid and fluid phases, which we denote by
\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}
and
\phi_{R_{\mathrm{f}}}
, respectively:

\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}=J_{\mathrm{s}}\phi_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{R_{\mathrm{f}}}=J_{\mathrm{f}}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to7.19pt{\vbox to7.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.59453pt\lower-3.59453pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.7 0 C 4.7 2.59 2.59 4.7 0 4.7 C -2.59 4.7 -4.7 2.59 -4.7 0 C -4.7 -2.59 -2.59 -4.7 0 -4.7 C 2.59 -4.7 4.7 -2.59 4.7 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(20)

Remark 3.1

(Solid immersed in fluid)
.

In the present kinematic framework, it is admissible for
\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}
to vanish over parts of
B_{\mathrm{s}}
. In such cases, the support of
\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}
will be assumed to be a closed manifold that supports the physics of a solid body immersed in a fluid.

3.2
Balance of momentum

The mixture is assumed to be isothermal. Using Refs.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 and
\refcite
Bowen1980Incompressible-0 as guides, the balance of momentum for phase
a=\mathrm{s},\mathrm{f}
in a mixture requires that in
B(t)
away from
\mathcal{S}(t)
we have

\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\nabla\!\cdot\!\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{a}-\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{a}-\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{a}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},

(21)

where
\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{a}
is the Cauchy stress of species
a
,
\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{a}
is an external force density (per unit volume of
B(t)
), and
\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{a}
is the force density (per unit volume of
B(t)
) acting on
a
because of its interaction with the rest of the mixture. The interaction forces must satisfy the constraint

\sum_{a}\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{a}=\bm{\mathbf{0}}.

(22)

For the momentum balance jump condition over
\mathcal{S}(t)
we posit that

\llbracket\rho_{a}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\otimes(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})-\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{a}\rrbracket\,\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},

(23)

where
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
is a force density per unit area of
\mathcal{S}(t)
analogous to
\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{a}
, and for which we posit a similar constraint

\sum_{a}\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}=\bm{\mathbf{0}}.

(24)

Equation (
23
) differs from what can be found in Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0 (cf. Eq. (2.10.22) in Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0) because of
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
. In Remark
4.5
of Section
4.1
, we argue that
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
plays a role in modeling dissipation at the interface and in the satisfaction of the first of Eqs. (
18
).

Equations (
23
) and (
24
) yield the following conditions for the solid, fluid, and mixture, respectively:

\displaystyle-\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{s}}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad d\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{f}}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},

(25)

and

\displaystyle d\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}+\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},

(26)

where
d
is

d\coloneqq\rho_{\mathrm{f}}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\llbracket d\rrbracket=0.

(27)

Remark 3.2
.

The jump condition in Eq. (
26
) is generally agreed upon in the literature (cf. Eq. (11
b
) in Ref.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit, Eq. (2.73) in Ref.
\refcite
Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas, and Eq. (30) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0). However, the same cannot be said for Eqs. (
25
). The tangential projection of the second of Eqs. (
25
) is similar to Eq. (32) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0.

3.3
Constitutive response functions

We assume that the pure solid phase is incompressible and hyperelastic. The strain energy of the pure solid phase is assumed to be a non-negative scalar function of the form

\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}=\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}(\overline{\text{$\mathsf{C}$}}_{\mathrm{s}},\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}}),

(28)

where

\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\coloneqq\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\text{$\mathsf{C}$}}_{\mathrm{s}}=J_{\mathrm{s}}^{-2/3}\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}},

(29)

so that
\overline{\text{$\mathsf{C}$}}_{\mathrm{s}}
is the isochoric part of
\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
. The strain energy of the solid phase in the mixture is simply assumed to that of the pure solid scaled by its volume fraction:

\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}\coloneqq\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}.

(30)

It is convenient to introduce a symbol to refer to the solid’s strain energy per unit volume of the current configuration:

\psi_{\mathrm{s}}\coloneqq\bigl(J_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}\bigr)^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(31)

The contributions to the first Piola-Kirchhoff and Cauchy stress tensors originating from the elastic strain energy are, respectively,

\text{$\mathsf{P}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{e}=2\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{e}=\biggl(2\phi_{R_{\mathrm{s}}}J_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\frac{\partial\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\biggr)^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(32)

In addition to a hyperelastic contribution, we allow for a dissipative stress contribution due to the interaction of the solid with the fluid phase, analogous in form to what is found in Eq. (2.1.3) in Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0. With this in mind, for
a=\mathrm{s},\mathrm{f}
, we define

\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{a}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}(\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}+\text{$\mathsf{L}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}_{a}),\quad\text{with}\quad\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}\coloneqq\nabla(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}).

(33)

Then, the viscous contribution to the solid’s stress in the mixture will be

\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{v}=2\mu_{B}(\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{s}}-\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}).

(34)

This type of contribution is common in poroelasticity, although it is usually discussed in relation to the fluid’s behavior where it is referred to as a Brinkman term.
[
13
]
The dynamic viscosity
\mu_{B}
is generally a function of
\phi_{\mathrm{s}}
with support identical to that of
\phi_{\mathrm{s}}
.

As far as the pure fluid phase is concerned, having already assumed that it is incompressible, we further assume that it is linear viscous, i.e., with viscous stress equal to
2\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{f}}\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}
, where
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{f}}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
is a constant dynamic shear viscosity. As for the stress response of the fluid in the mixture, we will assume that it consists of two terms: one with form
2\mu_{\mathrm{f}}\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}
, where
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}
is a dynamic viscosity that depends on
\phi_{\mathrm{f}}
, and the other being the counterpart of the Brinkman term introduced earlier (again, cf. Eq. (2.1.3) in Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1976Theory-of-Mixtu0):

\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}^{v}=2\mu_{\mathrm{f}}\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}+2\mu_{B}(\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}-\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{s}}).

(35)

The dynamic viscosity
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}
will be subject to intuitive constraints such as
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}\to 0
for
\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\to 0
and
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}\to\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{f}}
for
\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\to 1
.

A pressure field (cf., e.g., Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1980Incompressible-0) denoted by
p
and called the
pore pressure
is introduced over
B(t)
to enforce Eq. (
16
). Then, the full forms of
\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}
are, respectively,

\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{s}}=-\phi_{\mathrm{s}}p\,\text{$\mathsf{I}$}+\text{$\mathsf{T}$}^{e}_{\mathrm{s}}+\text{$\mathsf{T}$}^{v}_{\mathrm{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}=-\phi_{\mathrm{f}}p\,\text{$\mathsf{I}$}+\text{$\mathsf{T}$}^{v}_{\mathrm{f}},

(36)

where
\mathsf{I}
is the identity tensor.

The constitutive equations for a mixture must also include expressions for
\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{a}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
(
a=\mathrm{s},\mathrm{f}
). In both cases, we will assume that these forces originate from a viscous resistance to the sliding of the fluid relative to the solid, i.e., they originate from the kinematic quantity
(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}})
.

For
\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{\mathrm{f}}
(by Eq. (
22
),
\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{\mathrm{s}}=-\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{\mathrm{f}}
), we follow Ref.
\refcite
Bowen1980Incompressible-0 and write

\bm{\mathbf{p}}_{\mathrm{f}}=p\nabla\phi_{\mathrm{f}}+\bm{\mathbf{f}}_{\mathrm{f}},\quad\text{with}\quad\bm{\mathbf{f}}_{\mathrm{f}}=-\frac{\mu_{D}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}}{\kappa_{\mathrm{s}}}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}),

(37)

where
\kappa_{\mathrm{s}}
is the solid’s permeability, while
\mu_{D}
is a dynamic viscosity that, just like
\mu_{B}
, has support identical to that of
\phi_{\mathrm{s}}
. We defer the modeling of
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
to later in the paper.

Remark 3.3

(Inherent vs. mixture constitutive behavior)
.

In Mixture Theory, one distinguishes the inherent behavior of a phase from how a phase behaves in the presence of another. This fact is reflected in Eqs. (
34
), (
35
) (second term on the right-hand side), and the second of Eqs. (
37
), where we find well-known models for the fluid-solid
interaction
proposed by Brinkman
[
13
]
and Darcy
[
17
]
. When there is no interaction (see also discussion in Section 5.1 of Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0), the constituents’ response reverts back to the one they have in their pure form. The consequences of this fact can be counter-intuitive. For example, in the traditional Darcy flow model one has an otherwise
inviscid fluid
(
\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{f}}=0
and
\mu_{B}=0
), which takes on a viscous response (
\mu_{D}>0
) when flowing through a solid. The fact that the fluid model in the traditional Darcy model is inviscid is apparent in that the equations of motion do not provide a way to prescribe the boundary value of the tangential component of the fluid’s velocity. This lack of control is also reflected in the classic choice of functional setting for the fluid’s velocity, which is typically
H(\text{div})
for the traditional Darcy problem (cf., e.g., Refs.
\refcite
Masud2002A-Stabilized-Mixed-0 and
\refcite
Hu2017A-Nonconforming) rather than
H^{1}
as one finds in the Stokes or Navier-Stokes problems.
[
54
,
56
,
55
,
39
,
21
]

3.4
Additional consequences of momentum balance

Here we derive an energy estimate following a process similar to that used in the
TPE
(
TPE
).
[
22
,
24
]

We consider a time independent domain
\Omega\subset B(t)
such that it is crossed by the singularity surface
\mathcal{S}(t)
. Letting
\mathcal{S}(t)_{\Omega}\coloneqq\Omega\cap\mathcal{S}(t)
, it is assumed that
\mathcal{S}(t)_{\Omega}
is an open surface that separates
\Omega
into two domains
\Omega^{+}(t)
and
\Omega^{-}(t)
, such that the unit normal
\bm{\mathbf{m}}
is outward relative to
\Omega^{-}(t)
. Next, we take the inner product between Eq. (
21
) and
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}

(38)

where
\bm{\mathbf{n}}
is the boundary outward unit normal and

is the kinetic energy density of species

per unit volume of the mixture, i.e.,
