\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\|^{2}\rrbracket.

(42)

The quantities
\mathcal{W}_{\text{ext}}(\Omega)
,
\mathscr{D}
, and
\mathscr{I}
denote, respectively, the external supply of mechanical power to
\Omega
, the volume density of the dissipation rate in
\Omega
, and the surface density of the power expenditure on the interface. Then, summing the contributions in Eq. (
38
) for the fluid and the solid phases, and using the constitutive response functions for the stress in both the solid and fluid phases, for the mixture, we have (see Proposition
B.5
in the Appendix)

\mathcal{W}_{\text{ext}}(\Omega)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}(k_{\mathrm{s}}+k_{\mathrm{f}}+\psi_{\mathrm{s}})+\int_{\Omega}\mathscr{D}+\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\mathscr{I}.

(43)

Remark 3.4

(Theorem of power expended)
.

Equation (
43
) is the version of the theorem of power expended (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Gurtin-CMBook-1981-1) for the present context. It presents a consequence of the momentum balance laws as opposed to, say, the balance of energy. In a way, the result in Eq. (
43
) is intuitive: the right-hand side tells us what happens to the power input to
\Omega
, which is the left-hand side. It also allows to consider the power expended at the interface in a transparent manner, as argued in the following remarks.

Remark 3.5

(Inert vs. purely dissipative interfaces)
.

We call
inert
an interface which neither reversibly stores nor does it dissipate power. In this case, appealing to the arbitrariness of
\Omega
, the term
\mathscr{I}
in Eq. (
43
) must vanish:

\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}\cdot(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\rrbracket-\tfrac{1}{2}d\llbracket\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\|^{2}\rrbracket=0.

(44)

Provided that the constitutive assumptions in the present work are different from those in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0,
mutatis mutandis
, Eq. (
44
) coincides with Eq. (33) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 when the interface is inert. By contrast, if the interface were purely dissipative, then
\mathscr{I}
would
measure the dissipation at the interface:

\mathscr{I}=\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)},

(45)

where
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}
is the dissipation rate per unit area of the interface. This conclusion again agrees with Eq. (33) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, which does include dissipation.

Remark 3.6

(Development of a constitutive theory for the interface forces)
.

The result in Eq. (
43
) would be an important element in developing a constitutive theory of the interface. In this particular treatment, having assumed that the motion is
C^{1}_{\text{pw}}
and treating
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}
as globally continuous, the development of a constitutive relation for
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
would start with positing that
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
can be a function of the kinematic variables that experience a discontinuity at the interface, e.g., something like
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}=\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}(\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket,\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{D}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket,\ldots)
. Another possible strategy is that presented in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, where a constitutive assumption is made about the dissipation. For the purpose of obtaining a consistent formulation that can be more readily translated into a
FEM
, we will follow the latter strategy.

Remark 3.7

(Concerning the continuity of the pore pressure across
\mathcal{S}(t)
)
.

As articulated in Ref.
\refcite
Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas, in some cases, it is quite reasonable to expect the pore pressure
p
to be continuous across
\mathcal{S}(t)
. However, there are also cases in which a proper treatment of osmosis requires the possibility that the pore pressure be discontinuous across
\mathcal{S}(t)
. It is our position that if the constitutive behavior of the interface implies
\llbracket p\rrbracket=0
, the enforcement of this condition should arise naturally from the overall model formulation. In the next Section, we present a formulation based on Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 with this feature. To exemplify this point, suppose that we have an inert interface so that Eq. (
44
) holds. Suppose further that the pure fluid is inviscid as for the familiar Darcy flow, i.e.,
\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}=-p\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\text{$\mathsf{I}$}
. Finally, suppose that the inertia of the fluid is negligible. In this case, Eq. (
44
) reduces to

\llbracket p\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}\cdot(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\rrbracket=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\llbracket p\rrbracket(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}})=0,

where we have used the fact that the normal component of the filtration velocity is continuous as a consequence of the balance of mass. If
(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{m}})\neq 0
, we would then conclude that
\llbracket p\rrbracket=0
. This result is a “classic” result (cf., e.g., Ref.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit) that is recovered in the current treatment without enforcing it strongly by representing
p
as a globally continuous field.

4
A Variational Formulation

Here we derive the field equations using Hamilton’s principle (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Bedford2021Hamiltons-Princ) following the approach in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0. The use of Hamilton’s principle in poroelasticity is not new (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Bedford2021Hamiltons-Princ). Our purpose in somewhat duplicating the derivation of the field equations is (
i
) to provide a connection between the Newtonian approach (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Gurtin2000Configurational0) to derive the field equations and a variational one; and (
ii
) to find a simple path to their numerical implementation using
FEM
. As mentioned in Remark
3.6
, we wish to translate assumptions on the interface constitutive response to energy estimates for our
FEM
formulation in a direct way.

4.1
Hamilton-Rayleigh Principle

In the chosen kinematic framework, the system’s configuration can be equivalently described by the pair
\{\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}},\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{f}}\}
(cf. Eq. (
1
)) or the pair
\{\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}},\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\}
(cf. Eq. (
6
)). Recalling that the inverse image of the interface
\mathcal{S}(t)
under
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}},t)
is time independent, and observing that each element of
\{\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}},\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\}
has the same spatial domain
B_{\mathrm{s}}
and is continuous over it, we choose

q(t)\coloneqq\{\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}},\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\}

(46)

as the (abstract) motion of our system for the purpose of applying Hamilton’s principle.

Letting
\dot{q}\coloneqq\partial_{t}q
, and interpreting differentiation with respect to
q
and
\dot{q}
in a variational sense, for any sufficiently smooth
q
in time and
\delta q
with compact support in the time interval
[0,T]
, we require

\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\partial(\mathscr{K}-\mathscr{U})}{\partial q}\delta q+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\partial(\mathscr{C}+\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{flt}}-\mathscr{R})}{\partial\dot{q}}\delta q=0,

(47)

where, assuming for simplicity (and without loss of generality) that the boundary conditions consist of homogeneous Dirichlet data for both components of the motion,
\mathscr{K}
,
\mathscr{U}
,
\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{flt}}
, and
\mathscr{R}
are the following functionals:

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{B_{\mathrm{s}}}(k_{\mathrm{s}}+k_{\mathrm{f}})^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}J_{\mathrm{s}},

(48)

\displaystyle\mathscr{U}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{B_{\mathrm{s}}}(\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}-\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}J_{\mathrm{s}}-\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}J_{\mathrm{s}}),

(49)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{B_{\mathrm{s}}}p^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

(52)

\mathscr{K}
and
\mathscr{U}
are the system’s kinetic and potential energy, respectively.

\mathscr{C}
encodes constituent’s (individual) incompressibility, whereas letting

\mathfrak{p}:\mathcal{S}(t)\to\mathbb{R}

(53)

be a Lagrange multiplier,
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{flt}}
encodes the requirement that the normal component of the filtration velocity be continuous across
\mathcal{S}(t)
.
\mathscr{R}
is the time rate of energy dissipation. The term
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}
represents the interface dissipation mentioned in Remark
3.5
, for which we adopt the following (simple) quadratic form:

\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\|\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket\|^{2},

(54)

with
\mu_{\mathcal{S}}
a positive constant with dimensions of a dynamic viscosity per unit length.

Remark 4.1

(On the constitutive response of the interface)
.

The choice in Equation (
54
) is intended as an extremely simple example of how the constitutive response of the interface is built into the formulation. In this case we are positing that the interface is purely dissipative. As always, different choices for
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}
are possible, depending on the available kinematic framework. In fact, by properly modifying
\mathscr{U}
in Eq. (
49
), one can also describe interfaces capable of storing energy reversibly. Going back to Eq. (
54
),
recalling that the continuity of the normal component of the filtration velocity is enforced through the jump condition of the balance of mass, the chosen expression of
\mathscr{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}
can be interpreted as a penalization of
\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket
in the tangential direction. In turn, this provides a strategy for the weak enforcement of the full continuity of the filtration velocity, a condition discussed, e.g., in Refs.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit and
\refcite
Shim2022A-Hybrid-Biphas. This said, we note that we cannot expect that the chosen form of energy dissipation will yield a direct control of the jump discontinuity of the pore pressure, simply because we did not include terms designed to achieve such control. To some extent, our chosen model is also intended to illustrate this point. That is, if the jump discontinuity of the pore pressure is to be controlled, then a specific constitutive mechanism responsible for such control must be posited. The last example in Sec.
5
will further illustrate this point by showing that increasing the value of
\mu_{\mathcal{S}}
does yield a decrease of the norm of the jump of the tangential component of the filtration velocity but it does not have a similar control on the norm of the jump of the pore pressure.

Applying standard methods, under the stated assumptions (cf. also Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0), Eqs. (
47
)–(
54
) yield the following result (cf. Proposition
B.9
in the Appendix):

Remark 4.2

(Concerning Eqs. (
56
) and (
57
))
.

Referring to Eqs. (
21
) and (
22
), one can see that Eq. (
56
) expresses the balance of momentum for the mixture, the latter obtained by summing Eq. (
21
) for the two constituents and enforcing the constraint in Eq. (
22
); whereas Eq. (
57
) is directly the expression of the balance of momentum for the fluid phase. These equations correspond to Eqs. (28) and (29) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0.

Remark 4.3

(Concerning Eq. (
58
))
.

Equation (
58
) expresses the jump condition of the balance of momentum for the mixture as a whole. This equation coincides with Eq. (
26
).

Remark 4.4

(Concerning Eq. (
4.1
))
.

Equation (
4.1
) corresponds to Eq. (31) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0 and it can be treated following the arguments presented therein to break up the equation into components. The object on the left-hand side of Eq. (
4.1
) takes values in the cotangent space of points on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
. One component of this quantity can be obtained by taking a convenient tangent vector to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
acting on it. To select such a vector, we recall that the continuity of
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
implies:

\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}_{\mathrm{s}_{1}}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}_{\mathrm{s}_{2}}=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad\llbracket J_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle-\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad\text{and}\quad\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathbf{0}}.

(61)

Furthermore, under the kinematic framework in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, the following conditions also hold:

\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{f}}^{-1}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}_{\mathrm{s}_{1}}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{f}}^{-1}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}_{\mathrm{s}_{2}}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathbf{0}},\quad\text{and}\quad\llbracket J_{\mathrm{f}}^{-1}J_{\mathrm{s}}(\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{f}}^{-1}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}})^{\scriptscriptstyle-\mathrm{T}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}_{\mathrm{s}}\rrbracket=\bm{\mathbf{0}}.

(62)

Then, letting
\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}
be a tangent vector at
\hat{\bm{\mathbf{x}}}\in\mathcal{S}(t)
, a suitable tangent vector to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}(t)
would be
\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}
and its action on the left-hand side of Eq. (
4.1
), once remapped on
\mathcal{S}(t)
, yields

\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}\cdot\Bigl(d\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket\Bigr)=0\quad\text{on $\mathcal{S}(t)$}.

The above equation coincides with the projection of the second of Eqs. (
25
) along
\bm{\mathbf{\tau}}
. Letting
\mathbb{P}_{\tau}=\text{$\mathsf{I}$}-\bm{\mathbf{m}}\otimes\bm{\mathbf{m}}
denote the projection tangent to
\mathcal{S}(t)
, the above equation can then be given in the more general form

\mathbb{P}_{\tau}\Bigl(d\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\rrbracket-\llbracket\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket\Bigr)=\bm{\mathbf{0}}\quad\text{on $\mathcal{S}(t)$}.

(63)

Equation (
63
) is useful in providing clarity as to the nature of the surface force
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
introduced earlier. Clearly
\tfrac{1}{2}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{P}_{\tau}\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket
is only a component of
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
. As pointed out in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0, if
d\neq 0
, another (independent) component can be derived by considering the action of the vector
\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}-\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
(cf. the last of Eqs. (
8
)) onto the left-hand side of Eq. (
4.1
). The vector in question is continuous across
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
and not tangent to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{f}}
when
d\neq 0
.

Remark 4.5

(On the force
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
)
.

The force
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
was introduced in Eq. (
23
). We now present an argument for the determination of an explicit form for
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{a}
. We begin by observing that the result in Eq. (
57
) is derived from the second line of Eq. (
55
) by choosing an arbitrary variation
\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}
and relying on the fact that
\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}
are invertible. By contrast, Eq. (
4.1
) does not allow us to proceed in the same manner, but requires careful scrutiny on the freedom with which one can take variations of the motion of the fluid relative to the solid. This said, choosing the motion of the fluid relative to the solid to be discontinuous then the test function

\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}^{-1}\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}

can be given arbitrary discontinuous values across the interface. Clearly, taken in isolation, this statement can imply some unphysical situations. However, the overall effect of this assumption is tempered by having imposed the constraint in Eq. (
18
) explicitly through the term in Eq. (
51
). It is our view that this way of proceeding is similar to the imposition of the incompressibility constraint in, say, the mixed weak formulation for the Stokes problem (cf. Ref.
\refcite
Brezzi1991Mixed-and-Hybrid-0) where the velocity field and associated test functions are taken in spaces that are not necessarily divergence-free. This greater freedom in the choice of velocity function spaces is corrected by introducing a Lagrange multiplier that has the responsibility to enforce the divergence-free constraint. In our application, the Lagrange multiplier entrusted with ensuring conformance to the jump condition of the balance of mass is
\mathfrak{p}
.

With our choice of test functions, then the following result is obtained:

\Bigl(k_{\mathrm{f}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}-d\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.65pt{\vbox to6.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.32597pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.33 0 C 4.33 2.39 2.39 4.33 0 4.33 C -2.39 4.33 -4.33 2.39 -4.33 0 C -4.33 -2.39 -2.39 -4.33 0 -4.33 C 2.39 -4.33 4.33 -2.39 4.33 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.90556pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.64 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{p}\bm{\mathbf{m}}+\text{$\mathsf{T}$}_{\mathrm{f}}\bm{\mathbf{m}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket\Bigr)^{\pm}=\bm{\mathbf{0}}\quad\text{on $\mathcal{S}(t)$}.

(64)

We note that, under the assumption that
d=0
, Eqs. (
64
) have similarities with Eqs. (35) in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0. Next, referring to the second of Eqs. (
25
), using Eqs. (
64
), we would conclude that

\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{f}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{\mathcal{S}}\llbracket\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\rrbracket\llbracket\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{flt}}\rrbracket-\llbracket k_{\mathrm{f}}+\phi_{\mathrm{f}}\mathfrak{p}\rrbracket\bm{\mathbf{m}}.

(65)

The above form of
\bm{\mathbf{\xi}}_{\mathrm{f}}
is such that the first term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
65
) is tangent to the interface whereas the second term is normal to it.

4.2
A practical weak formulation

Here we propose a weak formulation of the problem based on Eq. (
55
) and the kinematic framework adopted in Remark
4.5
. For the purpose of this paper, we will use the same simple constitutive assumptions made in the discussion of Hamilton’s principle. Clearly, these assumptions can be changed to fit the modeling needs of a particular problem.

With the above in mind, the principal unknowns of our problem are selected as follows:

\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}},\quad\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\hbox to6.17pt{\vbox to6.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.08379pt\lower-3.08379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.99 0 C 3.99 2.2 2.2 3.99 0 3.99 C -2.2 3.99 -3.99 2.2 -3.99 0 C -3.99 -2.2 -2.2 -3.99 0 -3.99 C 2.2 -3.99 3.99 -2.2 3.99 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.58403pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.19 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}.

(66)

This choice of principal unknowns is meant to produce a formulation that is close to familiar
FEM
formulations of poroelasticity. That is, we do not perceive that a
FEM
in which the primary unknowns are
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}
would be adaptable to most applications. As for the particular form of the list,
Eq. (
66
) is meant to indicate that we are proposing an
ALE
(
ALE
) formulation where the field variables are understood to be defined over
B_{\mathrm{s}}
, the latter domain coinciding with our computational domain. At the same time, to avoid unnecessary repetition, we will keep using the same symbols as in the formulation section of the paper. Also, to avoid proliferation of symbols, we denote the test functions of said fields by a tilde.

While the regularity of these fields has been discussed earlier in the paper, we recognize that the formal properties of these fields must be examined on a case by case basis depending on the specific set of constitutive equations that are selected for a corresponding specific problem. In fact, the constitutive equations also have repercussions to the type of boundary conditions that one might impose, as it is clear from the comparison between, say, the traditional Stokes and Darcy problems. With this in mind, and referring to Eq. (
55
), we operate the following substitution of test functions:

\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}\to\tilde{\bm{\mathbf{u}}}_{\mathrm{s}}/\theta\quad\text{and}\quad-\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{sf}}^{-1}\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{sf}}\to\tilde{\bm{\mathbf{v}}}_{\mathrm{f}},

(67)

where
\theta
is a unit time constant whose only purpose is to achieve dimensional consistency. We then propose the following weak formulation of the problem, which incorporates a weak enforcement of the constraints in the second of Eqs. (
18
) and the first of Eqs. (
19
). For simplicity of presentation, we assume that Dirichlet boundary conditions are prescribed for
\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}
on the external boundary of the mixture. Such weak formulation is then given by

Proposition 4.6

(Formal stability estimate)
.

t\in[0,T]
,
\bm{\mathbf{u}}_{\mathrm{s}}
,

,

(69)

Proof 4.7

(71)

\displaystyle+\int_{B(t)}\mathcal{D}^{\hbox to6.36pt{\vbox to6.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.17973pt\lower-3.17973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.12 0 C 4.12 2.28 2.28 4.12 0 4.12 C -2.28 4.12 -4.12 2.28 -4.12 0 C -4.12 -2.28 -2.28 -4.12 0 -4.12 C 2.28 -4.12 4.12 -2.28 4.12 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}+\int_{\mathcal{S}(t)}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{S}(t)}.

Using the results in Propositions
B.1
and
B.5
in Appendix
B
, Eq. (
69
) follows.

Remark 4.8

(Consistency of the energy estimate)
.

The energy estimate just proven is consistent with that in Eq. (
43
) when Eq. (
45
) is used and every supply of power to the system is suppressed. We note that, for any physically admissible formulation, and excluding the trivial case of rigid body motions, the last two terms on the left-hand side of Eq. (
69
) are positive for any motion of the fluid relative to the solid, thereby ensuring a monotonic decline of the sum of the kinetic, strain, and stored energies of the system.

Remark 4.9

(Analysis and
FEM
)
.

We are not aware of a formal analysis of this specific problem articulated in the language of Sobolev spaces. Nor are we aware of an analysis within the context of the
FEM
.
We have implemented Eq. (
68
) using standard
FEM
approaches typical of
ALE
formulations. The interface
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
was simply modeled in a conformal way, namely, it consisted of inter-element boundaries. The chosen interpolation functions are as follows:

•

2nd order Lagrange polynomials for the (globally continuous) displacement field.

•

2nd order Lagrange polynomials for the fluid velocity field. This field is not continuous across the interface.

•

1st order Lagrange polynomials for the field
p
, which is not continuous across the interface.

•

1st order Lagrange polynomials for the field
\mathfrak{p}
, which is defined only over the interface.

The appropriateness of this selection of finite element spaces is supported by a recent work by Barnafi
et al.
,
[
4
]
which, along with Ref.
\refcite
Bansal2024A-Lagrange-Mult, offers some guidance for a rigorous analysis of the present work.

5
Results

In this section, we present three sets of results. We begin by verifying our
FEM
implementation using the method of manufactured solutions.
[
46
]
We then present our analytical solution to a classic problem, to compare our results with existing ones. Finally, we solve an example problem to investigate the effect of the interface viscosity
\mu_{\mathcal{S}}
on the jumps in the tangential component of filtration velocity and the pore pressure.

The numerical implementation of the formulation presented in this paper was done in COMSOL Multiphysics
®
,
[
15
]
which has been used as a
FEM
programming environment. We used a monolithic solver. For time integration we used the available variable time step and variable order
BDF
(
BDF
) method.
[
42
]
We have already mentioned the specific interpolation used. Additional details for the results presented will be indicated in context.

5.1
Verification

To verify the formulation, we employed the method of manufactured solutions.
[
46
]
We consider the planar geometry displayed in Fig.
1
,

:
Two contiguous square poroelastic domains A and B used for the verification of the
FEM
implementation of our formulation through the method of manufactured solutions.

which consists of two contiguous poroelastic domains with reference configurations A and B, respectively, aligned vertically and whose material properties are presented in Table
1
.

Referring to Fig.
1
, the position
\bm{\mathbf{X}}
of points in the reference configuration is expressed through the coordinates
X
and
Y
. The position
\bm{\mathbf{x}}
of points in the deformed configuration is expressed through the coordinates
x=X+u_{\mathrm{s}_{X}}(X,Y,t)
and
y=Y+u_{\mathrm{s}_{Y}}(X,Y,t)
, where
u_{\mathrm{s}_{X}}
and
u_{\mathrm{s}_{Y}}
are the horizontal and vertical components of the displacement field.
The images of A and B under the motion will be denoted by
\mathrm{A}(t)
and
\mathrm{B}(t)
, respectively.
The interface’s reference configuration
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
is the segment with
X\in[0,L]
and
Y=L
. For both domains, the neo-Hookean hyperelastic material model
\hat{\Psi}_{\mathrm{s}}=\tfrac{1}{2}\mu_{R_{e}}\left(\bar{I}_{1}-3\right)
is chosen for the strain energy density of the pure solid phase, where
\bar{I}_{1}
is the first principal invariant of
\bar{\text{$\mathsf{C}$}}_{\mathrm{s}}=J_{\mathrm{s}}^{-2/3}\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
, namely the isochoric component of the right Cauchy-Green strain tensor
\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
.
\mu_{R_{e}}
is the referential solid’s elastic shear modulus. Moreover, it is assumed that
\mu_{\mathrm{f}}=\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{f}}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
and
\mu_{B}=\bar{\mu}_{B}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}^{2}
.

The manufactured solution consists of the following fields:

where
\gamma=(\mu_{D}\phi_{\mathrm{f}}^{2})/\kappa_{\mathrm{s}}
,

,

,

,

, and
t_{o}=\numprint[s]{1}
. These fields are substituted in the strong form of the governing equations to calculate the source terms in the interiors of their domains of definition as well as on the interface.

The initial conditions are those dictated by the manufactured solution for
t=0
. For the solid part of the problem, the boundary conditions consist of prescribed displacements on the entirety of the external boundary; for the fluid part, traction boundary conditions are prescribed on the very top and bottom edges of the external boundary; the fluid’s velocity is prescribed on the lateral surfaces.

We have already indicated the choice of finite element spaces for our solutions. As far as the time integration part of the problem, we employed a
BDF
method of order 2 with a constant time step of
\numprint[s]{0.001}
.

The mesh employed was made up of identical triangles and was uniformly refined through four cycles. The initial mesh diameter was
h=\numprint[mm]{1.25}
.

Convergence rates for
3(a)
the

-norm and
3(b)

between numerical and exact solution in all variables, at time

, obtained using second-order Lagrange polynomials for the solid displacement and fluid velocity and first-order Lagrange polynomials for the pressure and the interface pressure. The computation of the norms of the relative errors is done via integrals that respect the domain of definition of said errors (e.g., the norms of the relative errors pertaining to
(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{A}})^{\hbox to6.47pt{\vbox to6.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\;\lower-3.23686pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.2 0 C 4.2 2.32 2.32 4.2 0 4.2 C -2.32 4.2 -4.2 2.32 -4.2 0 C -4.2 -2.32 -2.32 -4.2 0 -4.2 C 2.32 -4.2 4.2 -2.32 4.2 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.77779pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.46 -2.09)} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
are computed over
\mathrm{A}(t)
).

presents the convergence rates under uniform refinement. Specifically, we report the
L^{2}
-norm and the
H^{1}
-semi-norm of the relative errors for each of the quantities specified in the manufactured solution. As the functions indicated in Eqs. (
72
)–(
77
) each have their own domain of definition, the relative errors for these functions are computed via integrals that are consistent with said domains.

Provided that a formal analysis is not available so as to assess the optimality of the convergence rates, the convergence rates estimated here are rather good when compared to those typically obtained for the Navier-Stokes problem. We find this result rather encouraging, especially in view of the fact that we have relied on readily available numerical tools as opposed to devise custom-made algorithms for this problem.

5.2
A classic problem

To test our mathematical framework, we analytically solved a benchmark problem involving Poiseuille flow over a porous medium. In the Beavers-Joseph problem, a viscous fluid flows under a constant pressure gradient in a channel where the wall at the fluid-porous medium interface is permeable,
[
5
,
19
,
48
]
as illustrated in Fig.
4
.

Figure 4
:
Schematic view of viscous fluid flow over a rigid porous medium, driven by a constant pressure gradient.

The top and bottom boundaries of the system are assumed rigid and stationary, and the fluid velocity at these boundaries is set to zero. The flow inside the channel and the porous medium is only in the
x
direction. Among others, this problem was previously considered by Hou
et al.
[
27
]
in formulating boundary conditions at the interface between the pure fluid and the porous medium. In their study, they introduced a “pseudo-no-slip” kinematic interface condition based on the idea that the conditions at the interface between mixtures, or between mixtures and fluids, should reduce to those of single-phase continuum mechanics. They mentioned that, to formulate a well-posed mathematical problem for each subdomain of the fluid-solid interaction, it is necessary not only to satisfy the jump conditions for mass and momentum conservation, but also to impose additional kinematic constraints.
They chose to enforce the continuity of the pore pressure and the full continuity of the filtration velocity.

Here we solved the same problem but enforcing the jump conditions that were obtained in Eqs. (
64
).

We begin by observing that the unidirectional nature of the fluid flow and the rigidity of the porous medium imply that the mass balance is trivially satisfied. The dimensionless form of the balance of linear momentum can then be derived for both the fluid channel and the porous medium, and is given by, respectively,

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial^{2}V_{\mathrm{u}}}{\partial Z^{2}}+1=0,\quad

\displaystyle\text{for $0<Z<H$},

(78)

and

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\dfrac{\partial^{2}V_{\mathrm{l}}}{\partial Z^{2}}-V_{\mathrm{l}}+\eta\,\delta^{2}=0,\quad

\displaystyle\text{for $-1<Z<0$},

(79)

where the latter is the simplified version of Eq. (
57
) and the former is the dimensionless form of the Navier-Stokes equation in the absence of inertia and body forces.
Here,
V_{\mathrm{u}}(Z)
and
V_{\mathrm{l}}(Z)
are the dimensionless fluid velocities in the channel and the porous medium, respectively. Additionally,
Z=z/h_{2}
,
H=h_{1}/h_{2}
,
\eta=\phi^{2}_{\mathrm{f}}\mu_{\mathrm{f}}/\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
, and

\delta=\sqrt{\mu_{\mathrm{a}}/(h_{2}^{2}\kappa_{\mathrm{s}})}
are dimensionless parameters, where
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
represents the apparent viscosity of the fluid in the porous medium. Since the top and bottom boundaries are fixed and impermeable, the fluid velocity is zero at these boundaries. For the interface between the porous medium and the viscous fluid, the conditions represented in Eqs. (
64
) for the current problem can be simplified as

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{u}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma\left(V_{\mathrm{u}}-V_{\mathrm{l}}\right)=0,\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad Z\to 0^{+},

(80)

\displaystyle V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{l}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\gamma\eta\left(V_{\mathrm{u}}-V_{\mathrm{l}}\right)=0,\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad Z\to 0^{-},

(81)

where
\gamma=\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{S}}h_{2}}{\mu_{\mathrm{f}}}
is a dimensionless parameter representing the ratio of the interface viscosity to the fluid viscosity. Solving the governing equations along with the boundary conditions yields an analytical solution for the fluid velocity in both the channel and the porous medium.

(a)

H=1

(b)

H=0.25

Figure 5
:
Comparison of profiles of fluid flux in the porous medium and the fluid channel with
\eta=1
and
\gamma=1000
, for different values of
\delta
, and for (a)
H=1
and (b)
H=0.25
. The lines represent results according to the formulation presented in this paper whereas the hollow circles represent the solution in Ref.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit.

(a)

H=1

(b)

H=0.25

Figure 6
:
Comparison of profiles of fluid flux in the porous medium and the fluid channel with
\eta=2
and
\gamma=1000
, for different values of
\delta
, and for (a)
H=1
and (b)
H=0.25
. The lines represent results according to the formulation presented in this paper whereas the hollow circles represent the solution in Ref.
\refcite
Hou1989Boundary-condit.

Figures
5
and
6
compare our analytical solution and the analytical solution in Hou
et al.
[
27
]
Our solution is predicated on the use of a relatively large value of
\gamma
, which can be interpreted as representing a strongly dissipative interface. In this case, we show a strong agreement with the results reported by Hou
et al.
[
27
]
, who had enforced continuity of both the pore pressure and the entire filtration velocity across the interface as the implementation of what these authors call a “pseudo-no-slip” interface condition. With this result we show that the pseudo-no-slip condition, which is a kinematic constraint, can be viewed as the manifestation of a constitutive assumption on the interface behavior. Furthermore, we show that it is not necessary the continuity of the pressure in a strict sense to obtain the above results.

5.3
Role of interface viscosity

To investigate the role of the interface viscosity, we set up a numerical example with the geometry shown in Fig.
7
.

Figure 7
:
The example problem setup to study the effect of interface viscosity on the discontinuities of filtration velocity and pore pressure. The radius of domain B is
L/4
, where
L
has the same value as used in the verification problem.

The parameters used are reported in Table
1
, with the permeability of both subdomains increased by two orders of magnitude.

In this example problem, the top and bottom boundaries are impermeable, while the lateral boundaries are permeable and traction-free. The bottom boundary is fixed in the vertical direction, and a prescribed vertical displacement
u_{\mathrm{s}_{Y}}=u_{o}(t/t_{o})^{2}
is applied to the top boundary, with
u_{o}
and
t_{o}
having the same values used in the manufactured solution considered earlier.
Both the top and bottom boundaries are free to move in the horizontal direction. We suppress the rigid body motions of the domain A. Moreover, we adopt quiescent initial conditions.

, and

, at time

.

shows the effect of interface viscosity on the jump in the tangential component of the filtration velocity. As the interface viscosity increases, the norm of the jump of the tangential component of the filtration velocity decreases.

. Again, as the interface viscosity increases, the jump in the tangential component of the filtration velocity tends to zero, while the jump in the pressure converges to a non-zero value.

6
Summary, discussion, and conclusions

In this work we have proposed an approach to a variational formulation of the conditions at the interface separating two contiguous poroelastic domains. The formulation stems from mixture theory. We focus our attention to mixture of constituents that are incompressible in their pure form. This said, the present work is applicable to problems with large deformations. Our approach follows very closely that by dell’Isola
et al.
[
18
]
and adapts it to the constitutive class considered herein. We also offer a connection to a treatment of the subject according to a traditional continuum mechanics approach.
[
8
]

From a modeling perspective, our first goal was to make a more direct link between the constitutive assumptions pertaining to the behavior of the interface and the corresponding interface conditions. This is in contrast to interface conditions that rely on kinematic constraints such as the full continuity of the filtration velocity and/or of the pore pressure. The second goal of our work was to produce a variational formulation of the problem easily implementable as a
FEM
able to respect, at least formally, the energetics of the problem. In such an attempt, we chose test functions for the fluid velocity to have arbitrary jumps at the interface, while the constraint originating with the jump condition of the balance of mass is imposed by way of a Lagrange multiplier.

The results from the examples we have included are encouraging in that we have empirically shown convergence for our formulation using the method of manufactured solutions. In doing so, we have identified what seems to be a practical choice of stable finite element spaces.

We have also solved a classic problem of flow through a rigid porous medium showing that we can recover some well-established results by controlling the constitutive response of the interface. In our case, the formulation contains a dissipative term associated to the norm of the jump of the filtration velocity. This term can be interpreted as a penalization of the jump of the filtration velocity. As the value of the parameter
\mu_{\mathcal{S}}
is increased, our solution appears to approach a solution with continuous filtration velocity. The last set of results confirms this behavior, this time in a full poroelastic setting. However, it also shows that the jump of the pore pressure field across the interface is not directly controlled by the parameter that controls the jump of the filtration velocity. This conclusion was to be expected as these fields are indeed independent.

In conclusion, we believe that the proposed formulation has some encouraging results that warrant further development and analysis. As mentioned in the introduction, our interest lies in brain physiology and the modeling of membranes in the central nervous system and we plan future applications in this arena. These developments will require more sophisticated models to regulate the pore pressure jump across the interfaces under consideration. Another line of inquiry concerns the adaptation of the proposed
FEM
formulation to more general mixtures, say with compressible constituents, and with the kinematics considered in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0. From a numerical perspective, a formal investigation on stability issues associated to the compatibility of spaces of the Lagrange multipliers in relation to the other fields is necessary. An important area of study is also stability relative to constitutive parameters as discussed, for example, by Rodrigo
et al.
[
44
]

Appendix A
Notational conventions

A.1
Preliminaries

\mathbb{R}
will denote the real number set, with
\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
denoting its positive and non-negative subsets, respectively.
\mathcal{E}^{3}
will denote the three-dimensional Euclidean point space and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}
time. The translation (vector) space of
\mathcal{E}^{3}
will be denoted by
\mathscr{V}
. Given vector spaces
\mathscr{U}
and
\mathscr{W}
, the vector space of linear operators with domain
\mathscr{U}
and range in
\mathscr{W}
will be denoted by
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{U},\mathscr{W})
. The expression
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{V})
is shorthand for
\mathscr{L}(\mathscr{V},\mathscr{V})
.

Physical quantities are described by means of functions whose domain is a specific manifold. Often, the physical meaning of a quantity remains unchanged when the function representing it is composed with a map between a manifold and another. This fact is at the root of the distinction between Eulerian, Lagrangian, and Eulerian–Lagrangian frameworks (cf. Refs.
\refcite
GurtinFried_2010_The-Mechanics_0 and
\refcite
Fernandez2009The-Derivation-0) in Continuum Mechanics. To indicate the domain of a function representing one of these physical quantities, we adopt the shorthand notation in Ref.
\refcite
dellIsola2009Boundary-Condit-0. For any
t
, let
\Omega_{a}(t)\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
and
\Omega_{b}(t)\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
be two possibly time-dependent smooth manifolds whose points are denoted by
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
and
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{b}
, respectively. Let

such that

be a homeomorphism. Let

and

be continuous functions taking values in the vector spaces
\mathscr{U}
and
\mathscr{W}
, respectively. Then, we denote by

(82)

As in Ref.
\refcite
GurtinFried_2010_The-Mechanics_0, we view second-order tensors as linear operators from a vector space to another. Given a second-order tensor
\mathsf{A}
, its transpose will be denoted by
\text{$\mathsf{A}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}
. If
\mathsf{A}
is invertible, its inverse and its inverse transpose will be denoted by
\text{$\mathsf{A}$}^{-1}
and
\text{$\mathsf{A}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle-\mathrm{T}}
, respectively. Given a vector-valued quantity
\bm{\mathbf{u}}
in the domain of
\mathsf{A}
, the action of
\mathsf{A}
on
\bm{\mathbf{u}}
will be denoted by
\text{$\mathsf{A}$}\bm{\mathbf{u}}
.

Given a smooth manifold
\mathcal{A}
and a point
P\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}_{P}\mathcal{A}
denotes the tangent space at
P
. Clearly, in an Euclidean context, if
\mathcal{A}
is a three-dimensional submanifold of
\mathcal{E}^{3}
, its tangent spaces are isomorphic to
\mathscr{V}
. If a metric is defined over
\mathcal{A}
, the notation
\nabla\psi
will denote the gradient of the field
\psi
. We note that a gradient is not a stand-alone object in that it is inherently linked to the metric manifold over which it is defined — a gradient on a manifold is different from that on another manifold. The notation
\nabla\!\cdot\!\square
will denote the divergence of the quantity
\square
, assuming that
\square
admits a divergence. As is the convention in elementary calculus, we will use the notation
\partial_{t}\square
to denote the derivative of
\square
with respect to
t
while holding all other arguments of
\square
fixed.

Let a smooth surface
\Sigma
be a codimension-1 submanifold of a three-dimensional
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{E}^{3}
. Let
\bm{\mathbf{\nu}}
be a unit vector field orienting
\Sigma
. Let
\phi(\bm{\mathbf{X}})
,
\bm{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathcal{A}
, be some field that is continuous away from
\Sigma
. Then, at
\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}\in\Sigma
,

\phi^{\pm}\coloneqq\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\phi(\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}\pm s\bm{\mathbf{\nu}}),\quad\llbracket\phi\rrbracket\coloneqq\phi^{+}-\phi^{-},\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\phi\rangle\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}(\phi^{+}+\phi^{-}).

(83)

Appendix B

Here we provide explicit derivations for some relationships that were referenced in the main text.

Proposition B.1
.

Under the assumptions stated in the main paper, we have

\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{a}=\partial_{t}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\right)+\nabla\!\cdot\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\right).

(84)

Proof B.2
.

We recall that
\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{a}
is the material acceleration of species
a
. Hence, in Eulerian form we have

\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{a}=\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}+\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}=\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}+\nabla\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}[\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}],

where we recall that
\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}=\nabla\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}
and we write
\nabla\square[\diamond]
to denote the action of the gradient of a field
\square
onto the field
\diamond
, assuming that the operation is meaningful for
\square
and
\diamond
. We also recall the following two identities (cf. Ref.
\refcite
GurtinFried_2010_The-Mechanics_0):

[i]

1.

Given smooth vector fields
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{u}}
,

\nabla(\bm{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{u}})=(\nabla\bm{\mathbf{w}})^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}[\bm{\mathbf{u}}]+(\nabla\bm{\mathbf{u}})^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}[\bm{\mathbf{w}}].

(85)

2.

Given a smooth scalar field
\phi
and a smooth vector field
\bm{\mathbf{w}}
,

\nabla\!\cdot\!(\phi\bm{\mathbf{w}})=\nabla\phi[\bm{\mathbf{w}}]+\phi\nabla\!\cdot\!\bm{\mathbf{w}}.

(86)

Finally, we recall that the balance of mass for species
a
in the absence of chemical reaction is

\partial_{t}\rho_{a}+\nabla\!\cdot\!(\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a})=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\partial_{t}\rho_{a}=-\nabla\!\cdot\!(\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}).

With the above in mind, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{a}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\rho_{a}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}+\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a})

\displaystyle=\rho_{a}(\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\partial_{t}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}+\text{$\mathsf{L}$}_{a}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\left(\partial_{t}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}+\grad{\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}}[\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}]\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\grad{\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}}[\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}]-(\partial_{t}\rho_{a})\tfrac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\right)+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\grad{\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}}[\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}]+\left(\nabla\!\cdot\!\rho_{a}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\right)\tfrac{1}{2}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\right)+\nabla\!\cdot\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\rho_{a}\|\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\|^{2}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{a}\right).

Proof B.4
.

Let
\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}
be the inverse image of
\Omega
under the solid’s motion
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
. We recall that
\Omega
is partitioned into two domains by the moving surface
\mathcal{S}(t)
and that
\partial\Omega_{\text{ext}}
, the external boundary of
\Omega
, is fixed. By contrast, in the solid’s reference configuration, the inverse image of
\mathcal{S}(t)
, namely
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{s}}
, is fixed, whereas
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{s}_{\text{ext}}}
, the inverse image of
\partial\Omega_{\text{ext}}
, is a moving surface with normal velocity
-\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\bm{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathrm{s}}
, where
\bm{\mathbf{n}}_{\mathrm{s}}
is the unit normal orienting
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{s}_{\text{ext}}}
, outward relative to
\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}
. In fact, let
\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}(t)
be a point on
\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{s}_{\text{ext}}}
. This point is convected by the solid’s motion to the
fixed
point
\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}(t),t)
on
\partial\Omega_{\text{ext}}
so that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}(\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}(t),t)=\bm{\mathbf{0}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\partial_{t}\hat{\bm{\mathbf{X}}}=-\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}^{-1}\bm{\mathbf{v}}_{\mathrm{s}}.

With the above in mind, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\psi_{\mathrm{s}}

across

to rewrite the term under bracket on the second line of the right-hand side.

To provide the form of the potential energy variation, we start with recalling that
\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}=\text{$\mathsf{F}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{T}}_{\mathrm{s}}\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
, with
\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}=\nabla\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}
. Hence, we have

\delta\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}=\frac{\partial\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathbin{:}\delta\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}~\Rightarrow~\delta\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}=2\text{$\mathsf{F}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\frac{\partial\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}}\mathbin{:}\nabla\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}}~\Rightarrow~\delta\Psi_{\mathrm{s}}=\text{$\mathsf{P}$}_{\mathrm{s}}\mathbin{:}\nabla\delta\bm{\mathbf{\chi}}_{\mathrm{s}},

(100)

where we have taken advantage of the symmetry of
\text{$\mathsf{C}$}_{\mathrm{s}}
.

9
), (

), and recalling that the terms
J_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
J_{\mathrm{s}}\bm{\mathbf{b}}_{\mathrm{f}}
are treated as externally controlled entities at fixed
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\bm{\mathbf{X}}_{\mathrm{f}}

As
\mathscr{C}
has been introduced in rate form, its variation is computed as
\delta\mathscr{C}=(\partial\mathscr{C}/\partial\dot{q})[\delta q]
. This gives
